--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the foraging mission first.

## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score DECREASES by 1 for each item from one source half (good), INCREASES by 1 for each item from the other half (bad)
- Robots must: find the correct source (one color), collect items, return to nest (white floor)
- Key: identify which wall color (BLUE or GREEN) corresponds to the good item
- Since we don't know which is good, we should try both and see which scores lower
- Robots need to: explore → find source → return to nest with item

**Strategy:**
- BLUE walls (clr=3) or GREEN walls (clr=2) indicate source halves
- WHITE floor = nest, BLACK floor = source area, GRAY = in between
- RED wall (clr=1) = nest side

**State machine logic:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward source (GoToColor black floor or explore)
- State 1: On black floor (source) - got item, now go to nest (GoToColor red wall or white floor)
- State 2: On white floor (nest) - delivered, go back to source

**Key insight:** Try targeting BLUE source specifically (to avoid GREEN if GREEN is bad), using GoToColor with blue (clr=3) when not carrying good item, GoToColor red (clr=1) when returning.

Let me design 14 machines covering survival (none yet - round 1), exploitation, and exploration:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify further.

## Reasoning

**Key insight:** The score is MINIMISED. One source half decreases score by 1 (good), the other increases by 1 (bad). Robots should:
1. Target ONLY the good source half (either BLUE or GREEN walls)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall)
3. Avoid the bad source half

**Critical uncertainty:** We don't know if BLUE or GREEN is the good source. I'll hedge by including both variants and also mixed strategies.

**State design:**
- State "go to source": GoToColor targeting BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) walls
- State "return to nest": GoToColor targeting RED (clr=1) wall
- Trigger return when on BLACK floor (picked up item)
- Trigger foraging when on WHITE floor (delivered item)

**Improvements over Round 2:**
- Add GoAwayColor from the bad source to avoid picking up wrong items
- Use higher probabilities on floor-color transitions (more reliable)
- Try explicit avoidance of one color while seeking the other
- Mix neighbor-based conditions to avoid crowding at source

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback from the rounds. I'll reason from first principles about what should work best and diversify systematically.

## Reasoning

**Core logic:**
- BLACK floor = source area (item pickup)
- WHITE floor = nest (item delivery)
- RED wall (clr=1) = nest side → GoToColor red to return
- BLUE walls (clr=3) or GREEN walls (clr=2) = source halves
- One half is good (score -1), one is bad (score +1)

**Key improvement ideas:**
1. The simplest effective machine: GoToColor(blue/green) → on black floor → GoToColor(red) → on white floor → repeat
2. Avoid the bad half by using GoAwayColor from one color while seeking the other
3. Use LED colors to signal state (helps with neighbor conditions potentially)
4. The transition from return state back to foraging should trigger on WHITE floor (delivered) not gray

**New strategies to try:**
- Robots that actively avoid one source color (GoAwayColor) while seeking the other
- 4-state machines with dedicated "avoid bad source" states
- Vary the probabilities on floor-color triggers more aggressively
- Try GoToColor white (nest) instead of GoToColor red for return trip

I'm not receiving score feedback between rounds, which makes it hard to do proper elitism/exploitation. I'll reason more carefully about what should work and try bolder designs.

## Reasoning

**Critical re-analysis:**
- Score is MINIMISED. One source half gives -1 (good), other gives +1 (bad).
- Without feedback, I need to cover both BLUE-good and GREEN-good scenarios equally.
- The fundamental loop: GoToColor(source_color) → black floor detected → GoToColor(red/nest) → white floor detected → repeat.
- The return trip: GoToColor RED wall (clr=1) is the nest side wall. Alternatively use floor=WHITE as the "delivered" trigger.

**What might be failing:**
- Robots might be oscillating near source without clearly committing
- The "go back" trigger might fire too early (gray floor can appear between source and nest)
- Maybe I need to use GoToColor(white) for return instead of GoToColor(red)

**New ideas:**
- Use clr=4 (yellow) which doesn't exist in arena → try clr=5 (magenta) or clr=6 (cyan) for LED signaling
- Try WHITE floor (clr=4 in GoToColor? No - clr is {1-6} for colored objects, not floor)
- Actually clr=4=yellow, clr=5=magenta, clr=6=cyan for wall blocks
- Use a 4-state machine: explore→found source(blue)→return to nest→delivered
- Make transitions more decisive with p=1.0 on floor conditions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm still not receiving score data. Let me think more carefully about what might be going wrong.

## Reasoning

**Deep re-analysis of the problem:**

The key issue I may have been missing: the score is MINIMISED, meaning robots should bring items from the GOOD half. But we don't know which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is good. However, the evolutionary optimiser will select whichever version scores lower. So I should keep both variants.

**What might be failing in my designs:**
1. The "return to nest" transition fires on WHITE floor - but robots start near center, so they might already be on gray and never reach black
2. GoToColor(RED) for return: RED walls are at the nest side - this should work
3. The transition `on WHITE floor → go back to source` is correct for "delivered, now fetch again"
4. But: what if robots reach WHITE floor WITHOUT having picked up an item? They'd loop needlessly.

**Better state machine logic:**
- State 0 (SEEK): GoToColor(BLUE) - approach blue source
- Transition: BLACK floor detected → go to State 1
- State 1 (RETURN): GoToColor(RED) - go back to nest  
- Transition: WHITE floor detected → go to State 0
- This is the minimal correct loop.

**The real problem might be:** transitions with p=1.0 on floor conditions may cause oscillation at boundaries. Better to use p=0.9-0.95.

**Also trying:** Using GoToColor for white floor approach directly (clr doesn't have white - white is floor color only, detected by C=2 condition). So return must use GoToColor(RED wall, clr=1).

Let me also try completely different structures with neighbor-based coordination.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm still not receiving score feedback. Let me fundamentally reconsider the problem.

## Deep Reasoning

**What I've been missing:** The score starts at 0. Every delivery from the GOOD half gives -1, every delivery from the BAD half gives +1. To get a very negative score, robots must make MANY deliveries from the good half and ZERO from the bad half.

**The simplest correct 2-state machine:**
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE) → transition on BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED) → transition on WHITE floor → State 0

This is clean and should work. The only question is BLUE vs GREEN.

**But wait:** When a robot is in State 1 (returning) and it transitions on WHITE floor back to State 0, it immediately starts going toward BLUE again. This is correct.

**Problem I may have overlooked:** When returning (GoToColor RED), if the robot crosses gray→white that's fine. But the WHITE condition fires at probability p each step while on white. With p=0.97, it fires quickly. Good.

**New insight:** Maybe I should try using the GRAY floor as a waypoint check, or use neighbor counts to avoid crowding at the source.

**Most important:** Since both BLUE-good and GREEN-good variants must be tried, and I get no feedback, I'll weight them equally. But I'll focus on the cleanest, simplest designs that execute the loop reliably.

**Also trying:** Exploration-based approach where robots just wander and use floor color to decide direction, without GoToColor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback. Let me try a completely different analytical approach.

## Reasoning

**Fundamental rethink:** I've been designing machines that I think should work logically, but without feedback I can't know which are actually performing. Let me think about failure modes more carefully.

**Possible critical bug in my designs:** When robots are in "return" state (GoToColor RED), and they happen to be near the source area still on BLACK floor, the BLACK floor condition won't fire (it's not a transition in state 1). They'll just head toward RED walls. That's fine.

**But:** When robots deliver and transition back to seeking (WHITE floor → State 0), they're now at the nest end. GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) should pull them back toward the source. This should work.

**A key concern:** What if the "good" source is actually identified by which LED color robots carry? No - the problem says entering a half gives an item of that type. The robot doesn't know which is good/bad.

**New structural idea:** What if I use robot LEDs as signals and neighbor detection? Robots returning (LED=yellow) could guide others toward the nest direction. Probably too complex.

**Most promising simple structure I haven't fully exploited:**
- Only 1 transition per state (minimal, clean, no conflicting conditions)
- GoToColor(BLUE) → on black → GoToColor(RED) → on white → repeat

Let me make ultra-clean variants with single transitions, plus some with 2 transitions adding a "camera sees color" backup trigger.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback provided. Let me try a completely different approach and think about what could be systematically wrong.

## Reasoning

**Critical insight I may have missed:** The problem says "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying." This means if a robot enters the BAD half while heading toward the GOOD half (because both are BLACK floor), it gets a BAD item. So GoToColor(BLUE) robots might accidentally enter the GREEN half on their way through.

**The arena layout:** Source area is at one end, divided into two halves side by side. BLUE walls behind one half, GREEN walls behind the other. So robots approaching from the center see both BLUE and GREEN walls. GoToColor(BLUE) should steer toward the blue-walled half specifically.

**Another critical insight:** Maybe the transition "on BLACK floor → return" is firing immediately when entering the wrong half too. The robot gets a bad item and returns with it. Score goes up.

**What if I use GoAwayColor(bad_color) as the seeking state?** While approaching source, repel from the bad color. But we don't know which is bad.

**Completely new idea:** What if robots use a 3-state machine where:
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE) - approach blue side
- State 1: GoToColor(RED) - return to nest  
- State 2: GoAwayColor(GREEN) - avoid green while in source area
- Transition: black floor → state 2 (if we entered wrong half, get pushed away) → state 1

Actually the key issue: once on black floor, item is acquired. Best to return immediately.

**Let me try targeting different colored walls for return:** Maybe using GoToColor for a specific LED color that other robots emit (yellow=4) for coordination.

**Most importantly:** Let me try completely different transition triggers - using camera perception of colors rather than floor colors.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
This is the final round. Without score feedback throughout, I need to make my best judgement about what works.

## Reasoning

**Final analysis:** After 9 rounds with no score data, I must commit to the designs I believe are most correct. The core logic is sound:
- GoToColor(source_color) → BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED nest wall) → WHITE floor → repeat

**What I know for certain:**
- clr=3 (BLUE walls) and clr=2 (GREEN walls) are the two source halves
- clr=1 (RED walls) marks the nest side
- BLACK floor = source, WHITE floor = nest, GRAY = transit
- Transitions C=0 (black), C=2 (white) are the key floor sensors

**The absolute simplest correct machine is 2-state:**
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN), transition on BLACK→S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED), transition on WHITE→S0

**For the final round, I'll:**
1. Submit multiple copies of the cleanest 2-state machines (both BLUE and GREEN variants) - these are the most likely to work
2. Submit 3-state variants with an intermediate exploration state
3. Submit a few 4-state machines with avoidance of the bad color
4. Vary probabilities slightly to cover parameter space

The key bet: one of BLUE or GREEN is the good color, and the 2-state machine targeting it will score very negatively (-many deliveries).

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
